  4. Farm: 439.8 days \(/\) 14 \(=\) 31.4 days, about a month.
  5. Wear: 1.0 × 10⁶ \(\times\) 4 \(=\) 4.0 × 10⁶ drivetrain load reversals, for any \(\ParallelRigCount\).

Result: Manual resets extend the campaign to more than a year on a single machine, and even a 14-rig farm requires an entire month of continuous operation.

Systems insight: Reset overhead dominates active execution time, and scaling out hardware rigs partitions the calendar without mitigating cumulative mechanical wear. Only reducing the interaction count \(\TrialCount\) relieves both calendar duration and physical fatigue. Consequently, algorithmic sample efficiency, rather than hardware parallelism, determines whether on-machine reinforcement learning is economically viable.

Adding parallel rigs scales floor space, capital cost, electrical power infrastructure, and supervisory labor in direct proportion to \(\ParallelRigCount\). A grasp-learning farm shows what that scale looks like (figure 4): up to 14 arms, each with its own gripper, bin, and camera, ran day and night, and the cluster collected over 800,000 grasp attempts over two months. The farm was not running reinforcement learning. It used self-supervised grasp outcomes to train an offline success predictor, which an external servo loop queried to guide real-time grasping motions (Levine et al. 2018). That is why it serves here as a picture of scale rather than of an algorithm. Whatever the learner, collecting close to \(10^6\) physical trials takes a room of arms running for months and pays the wear that comes with it. QT-Opt, which did learn its grasping by reinforcement learning, paid the same price for its 580,000 attempts.

Mechanical component wear often becomes the binding constraint long before calendar limits are reached. The cumulative 4.0 × 10⁶ load reversals demanded by a from-scratch campaign must be absorbed by actuator drivetrains regardless of how many rigs divide the trials.8 Those same million contact events progressively gall the latch cam and strike plate, wear out compliant elastomer gripper pads, and cycle internal wiring harnesses toward fatigue failure.

8 Bearing Life vs. Command Reversals: ISO 281 models rolling-bearing fatigue life under continuous rated loads and rotational velocities. Precision transmission manufacturers specify wave-generator bearing fatigue life (\(\RatedFatigueLife\)) in operating hours under nominal operating conditions. However, converting high-frequency commanded torque reversals into consumed gearhead life requires an empirical, unit-specific duty-cycle model.

In purely numerical simulation, an exploratory branch that destabilizes costs nothing more than a discarded buffer and an environment reset. On physical hardware, exploration answers to the first law, irreversibility (principle 1), because a trial that goes wrong delivers its energy before any gradient step can take it back. Unconstrained exploratory torque commands can slam mechanisms into rigid fixtures, strip the precision teeth of harmonic drives, and heat motor stators beyond their 155 °C Class F insulation limit.

↩︎ The Class F winding limit and the insulation breakdown it guards against are established in Thermal capacity and insulation breakdown thresholds.

Physical degradation systematically alters the plant throughout the training campaign. Standard Markov Decision Process formulations assume that the transition probability kernel \(\EnvironmentTransitionDistribution(\DecisionProcessState_{t+1} \mid \DecisionProcessState_{t}, \ContinuousActionVector_{t})\) remains strictly stationary. On physical hardware, this assumption fails. Repetitive impacts burnish contact surfaces, backlash in precision gearboxes expands, and structural fasteners loosen under continuous cyclic vibration. Consequently, the transition dynamics late in a campaign, \(\WornPlantTransitionDistribution(\DecisionProcessState_{t+1} \mid \DecisionProcessState_{t}, \ContinuousActionVector_{t})\), differ measurably from the pristine dynamics measured at trial \(\TrialIndex = 1\). The physical plant mutates under the mechanical work of collecting its own training data, forcing the optimizer to track a drifting target.

Reward formulations can also inadvertently incentivize destructive hardware behaviors. In trial-based policy search, the scalar reward \(\Reward(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector)\) defines the optimization objective, while the action parameterization, lower-level controllers, and independent hardware interlocks define the admissible operating space. If neither the reward nor those controllers account for contact forces, torque slew rates, or thermal buildup, the policy optimizer will exploit those unpenalized degrees of freedom to maximize task return. Engineers mitigate these tendencies by penalizing excessive effort, jerk, and temperature: \[\ShapedTotalReward(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector) = \TaskReward(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector) - \TorqueEffortPenaltyWeight \|\JointTorque_t\|_2^2 - \TorqueSlewPenaltyWeight \left\| \frac{\JointTorque_t - \JointTorque_{t-1}}{\FiniteChange \TimeInstant} \right\|_2^2 - \ThermalPenaltyWeight \max\left(0, \WindingTemperature - \WindingTemperatureLimit\right).\] For example, an unregularized tracking reward often encourages the policy to bang current limits back and forth at high frequency, exciting mechanical resonances and generating severe thermal dissipation. Similarly, an unpenalized contact policy may brace rigidly against a mechanical stop to artificially constrain kinematic degrees of freedom. The penalty weights above (\(\TorqueEffortPenaltyWeight, \TorqueSlewPenaltyWeight, \ThermalPenaltyWeight\)) trade task progress against effort, torque slew, and thermal rise. Soft reward penalties cannot guarantee safety, however. Hard physical stops and active current limits in the motor drive firmware must enforce strict operating envelopes. Practical on-machine training must also cut the million-trial budget by three orders of magnitude before wear and reset labor exhaust the workcell.

6.3.1 Demonstration-seeded reinforcement learning on the machine

Instead of spending 439.8 days of single-machine hardware wear on random exploration, human-in-the-loop sample-efficient reinforcement learning (HIL-SERL) achieves high success rates across precision assembly, dynamic manipulation, and dual-arm tasks after only 1 h to 2.5 h of physical training per task (Luo et al. 2025). Seeding exploration with prior demonstrations circumvents the million-trial reset wall by trading raw trial count for dependencies on initial demonstration support, human intervention gating, and reward-classifier accuracy. Four mechanisms compress the required trial count \(\TrialCount\). First, demonstration seeding initializes the replay buffer with 20 to 30 teleoperated trajectories, ensuring that early gradient updates begin within competent task regimes. Second, aggressive off-policy data reuse, implemented via Reinforcement Learning with Prior Data (RLPD) (Ball et al. 2023), replays each recorded transition across multiple gradient updates rather than limiting reuse to a recently collected rollout batch. Third, human-gated interventions truncate failing episodes; an operator takes over when the policy destabilizes, routing the corrective maneuver directly into the replay buffer as in human-gated DAgger (Kelly et al. 2019). Finally, an autonomous binary success classifier trained on labeled visual examples replaces brittle, hand-engineered reward instruments.

Ball, Philip J., Laura Smith, Ilya Kostrikov, and Sergey Levine. 2023. “Efficient Online Reinforcement Learning with Offline Data.” Proceedings of the 40th International Conference on Machine Learning, Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 202: 1577–94.
Kelly, Michael, Chelsea Sidrane, Katherine Driggs-Campbell, and Mykel J. Kochenderfer. 2019. “HG-DAgger: Interactive Imitation Learning with Human Experts.” IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 8077–83. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA.2019.8793698.

At the latch task’s per-trial duration with automated fixture resets, the 2.5 h upper ceiling permits at most 750 physical rollouts. Under these latch timing assumptions, that budget contains a factor of 1,300 fewer trials than the illustrative from-scratch campaign, with 3,000 load reversals rather than 4.0 × 10⁶, shifting the operational burden of equation 5 from months to hours. However, each sample-efficiency mechanism introduces assumptions that must be explicitly tracked in the policy manifest (section 6.8). Demonstrations constrain the policy only within the support of the expert’s initial distribution. Human interventions generate authority-record transitions whose pre-takeover states serve as negative failure indicators rather than positive imitation targets. The success classifier acts as a learned perceptual sensor, and the policy optimizer will exploit its systematic false positives, such as mistaking a partially jammed cam for an open door.

↪ The authority record, in which the permission path logs every takeover and barrier trip, and from which intervention episodes are curated, is developed in The Authority Record.

Even with demonstration seeding, direct on-hardware reinforcement learning fits only tasks whose interaction budget stays within the physical plant’s thermal endurance, fatigue life, and available calendar time. When broader exploration is needed and narrowing the task or adding stronger priors is insufficient, engineers offload rollouts to simulation. Moving exploration into a virtual workcell removes hardware wear and reset latency during training, but makes the learned policy vulnerable to every unmodeled dynamic, softened contact constraint, and missing transport delay in the simulator.

6.4 The Simulator as a System Component

A reinforcement learning policy trained on door latching learned an aggressive, unbraked plunge because its simulated strike plate yielded far more than the physical mechanism. Stripping away the simulator during physical deployment does nothing to unlearn that behavior. The physics simulator is not an external sandbox; it is an active component of the training pipeline whose equations, contact approximations, and timing assumptions dictate the exact experience supplied to the optimizer. Accepting simulated task success as evidence of hardware readiness requires measurable, verifiable error bounds on every one of those assumptions.

Like any physical subsystem, a simulator requires a formal interface contract stated in quantities that engineers can measure and budget. This interface contract specifies observation dimensions, action bounds, simulation time step \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\), transport latency between command issuance and physical actuation, actuator torque and current limits, sensor noise distributions, reset distributions, and termination conditions. For instance, if a simulator executes an observation loop at 100 Hz with an assumed zero-delay sensor channel and Gaussian noise of variance \(\SensorNoiseStandardDeviation^2 = 1.0 \times 10^{-4}\text{ (rad/s)}^2\), the policy tunes its feedback gains to those exact signal statistics. An unmodeled 15 ms bus transport delay on the physical machine then removes \(\CrossoverFrequency \PureTransportDelay\) of phase at the loop’s crossover frequency \(\CrossoverFrequency\) (equation 4.1), and the loop goes unstable whenever that loss exceeds the phase margin the learned gains left. OpenAI’s Dactyl project faced this contract at full scale, with a 24-DoF Shadow Hand in a motion-capture cage and its MuJoCo twin (figure 5) (Andrychowicz et al. 2020). The team calibrated the simulator against the physical hand and then randomized physical parameters around those calibrated values, so that the policy met the remaining discrepancies as variation it had already seen in training.

War Story 6.1: Sim-to-real hardware breakage (2018)
Context: OpenAI trained a reinforcement learning policy entirely in a randomized MuJoCo simulation to reorient a block in a 24-DoF Shadow Dexterous Hand, whose joints are driven through tendons by motors in its base (Andrychowicz et al. 2020).

Mechanism: The simulated hand applied torque directly at its joints, while the physical hand is tendon-actuated and has substantial backlash. The authors smoothed every action with an exponential moving average, in simulation and on the robot, to avoid abrupt changes in the action signal that could harm the physical hand.

Impact: The authors reported frequent hardware breakages during physical evaluation, concentrated at the wrist vertical joint under heavy load. Each repair altered tendon tension, joint friction, and sensor calibration, shifting the physical plant across trials and lengthening the evaluation campaign.

Response: Physical ablations revealed the mechanisms enabling successful transfer. Policies trained without domain randomization, or without modeling unmodeled hardware traits like drivetrain backlash and action delay, achieved far fewer consecutive rotations. Furthermore, mechanically locking the fragile wrist pitch joint produced substantially higher physical reliability.

Systems lesson: A simulator that neither wears nor breaks cannot price hardware exposure into the reward. The limits that protected the hand, an action filter and a locked joint, were imposed outside the learned policy, and every repair changed the plant on which the policy was being evaluated.

When a simulator uses an inaccurate rotor inertia, assigning \(\RotorInertia = 2.0 \times 10^{-3}\text{ kg}\cdot\text{m}^2\) instead of the true \(2.5 \times 10^{-3}\text{ kg}\cdot\text{m}^2\), the underlying equations of motion still contain the acceleration-dependent inertial-torque term, with the wrong magnitude. The parameter error alters the size of the response, but the causal relationship holds. In contrast, omitting an entire physical mechanism, such as drivetrain backlash, structural compliance, or bus communication jitter, removes that governing physical constraint entirely. An omitted delay or compliance represents an unmodeled physical degree of freedom. For instance, a simulator that treats robot joints as perfectly rigid permits instantaneous torque reversals. When commanded on a physical machine, those instantaneous reversals shock the drivetrain, load gear teeth beyond their fatigue limits, and excite destructive structural resonances.

Whether a simulator’s modeling compromises are acceptable depends on two properties: throughput (the rate of experience generated per wall-clock hour) and contact fidelity (how faithfully the contact model reflects physical strike-plate dynamics). Pure throughput motivates teams to adopt simulation in the first place.

6.4.1 Massively parallel simulation and on-policy learning

Stepping thousands of virtual workcells in parallel on a single GPU compresses months of trial-and-error into minutes, making on-policy methods such as proximal policy optimization (PPO) (Schulman et al. 2017) practical without continuous machine wear. Because PPO collects rollouts with the current policy, takes several gradient steps on that batch, and then discards the data, replacing the batch before policy drift makes its policy-ratio correction less reliable, it requires a simulator that produces fresh rollouts in bulk. To maintain training stability across those gradient steps, PPO constrains policy drift using the clipped surrogate objective of equation 6: \[\PpoClippedSurrogateObjective(\ModelParameters) = \EmpiricalTimestepAverage_t\left[\min\left(\PolicyProbabilityRatio_t(\ModelParameters)\,\EstimatedHistoryAdvantage_t,\ \operatorname{clip}\left(\PolicyProbabilityRatio_t(\ModelParameters), 1 - \RatioClippingWidth, 1 + \RatioClippingWidth\right)\EstimatedHistoryAdvantage_t\right)\right], \qquad \PolicyProbabilityRatio_t(\ModelParameters) = \frac{\Policy(\CognitivePolicyAction_{t} \mid \PhysicalWorldState_{t})}{\pi_{\RolloutPolicyParameters}(\CognitivePolicyAction_{t} \mid \PhysicalWorldState_{t})}, \tag{6}\] where \(\PolicyProbabilityRatio_t(\ModelParameters)\) measures the probability ratio between updated and rollout policies, and \(\RatioClippingWidth\) clips the surrogate incentive for favorable probability-ratio changes; it does not impose a hard trust-region bound on the updated policy. Once the first gradient step updates the network, the current policy no longer matches the rollout policy that collected the batch, so each logged transition is reweighted by the importance-sampling ratio \(\PolicyProbabilityRatio_t(\ModelParameters)\) between the two distributions. Taking the minimum of the unclipped and clipped terms removes the gradient incentive to push that ratio above \(1 + \RatioClippingWidth\) when the advantage \(\EstimatedHistoryAdvantage_t\) is positive or below \(1 - \RatioClippingWidth\) when the advantage is negative.

GPU-resident simulation achieves this scale by maintaining the state of thousands of parallel environments directly in accelerator memory. Physics solvers advance all environments concurrently using batched GPU compute kernels, streaming observation tensors directly into the policy network without crossing the PCIe bus to host CPU memory (Makoviychuk et al. 2021). Policy inference evaluates concurrently across the entire population, turning an environment step into a deterministic sequence of GPU kernel launches rather than thousands of separate CPU process calls.

Rudin and colleagues trained an agile quadruped locomotion policy using this architecture with 4,096 parallel simulated robots, each gathering 24 environment steps per update cycle to form a batch of 98,304 transitions. Completing 1,500 policy updates required less than 20 min on a single workstation GPU (Rudin et al. 2022). This run processed 1.47 × 10⁸ total policy steps, sustaining an aggregate throughput of at least 1.23 × 10⁵ steps per second and accumulating 34.1 days of simulated locomotion at the policy’s 50 Hz control frequency.

At reported throughput, simulation turns 138.9 days of automated trials into 43.4 min.

Applying that throughput to the latch scenario illustrates the appeal. The from-scratch training budget from section 6.3 requires 3.2 × 10⁸ policy steps. At the GPU simulator’s reported rate, this training run completes in 43.4 min, compared to 138.9 days on an automated physical test fixture.

While this thousandfold speedup explains why modern robotic reinforcement learning relies heavily on simulation, raw step throughput can easily mislead. The quadruped benchmark measured locomotion across smooth heightfield terrain. The authors noted that simulation execution time varied by a factor of two over the course of training. As the robots dispersed and ceased colliding with one another, computational cost dropped precipitously because narrow-phase contact detection and linear complementarity solving dominate simulator runtime. Manipulating a latch involves complex multi-body interactions (an articulated door, a sliding cam, and a high-stiffness strike plate) where contact resolution operates at its slowest, and rendering camera observations introduces additional memory and compute bottlenecks. Furthermore, published throughput benchmarks often sacrifice fidelity for speed. For example, the 2024 launch benchmark for one GPU physics simulator sparked controversy because it evaluated throughput by issuing a single action followed by open-loop stepping, disabling robot self-collisions, and using aggressive, low-accuracy solver settings (Tao 2024). High step rates provide no guarantee of physical validity. Training an optimizer against an unvalidated contact model drives the policy to exploit the simulator’s numerical artifacts, as demonstrated by the latch simulator’s contact response.

Tao, Stone. 2024. Simulation Speed Benchmark Likely Has Significant Issues Leading to Overstated Numbers. GitHub issue #181, Genesis-Embodied-AI/genesis-world.

6.4.2 The sim-to-real contact exploit

When a physics simulator softens a steel strike plate into a compliant penalty spring to keep its numerical integrator stable at large timesteps, a reinforcement learning policy will exploit that artificial cushion by plunging the gripper into the latch at 0.10 m/s without braking. Contact mechanics govern every phase of door manipulation: the physics solver must map surface separation, material penetration, friction, and relative velocity into normal and tangential forces observable by the policy. Rigid Body Contact Mechanics details rigid-contact complementarity formulations (Stewart and Trinkle 1996) and the time-stepping and compliant approximations that simulators substitute for them. The immediate engineering criterion for training the latch policy is whether these approximations preserve the contact forces and timing boundaries that govern safe approach speeds.

Stewart, David E., and Jeffrey C. Trinkle. 1996. “An Implicit Time-Stepping Scheme for Rigid Body Dynamics with Inelastic Collisions and Coulomb Friction.” International Journal for Numerical Methods in Engineering 39 (15): 2673–91. https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0207(19960815)39:15<2673::AID-NME972>3.0.CO;2-I.

Rigid-body physics engines typically model contact using one of two paradigms: constraint-based complementarity formulations, which enforce non-penetration by solving for constraint impulses, or penalty formulations, which permit geometric interpenetration and generate restoring forces proportional to penetration depth. In practice, real-time solvers approximate both approaches, and numerical tuning parameters alter the resulting dynamics. In the latch simulator, the penalty contact computes an instantaneous normal restoring force from penetration depth \(\PenetrationDepth\) and penetration velocity \(\PenetrationRate\) via \(\NormalContactForce = \PenaltyContactStiffness \PenetrationDepth + \PenaltyContactDamping \PenetrationRate\). Artificially softening this contact interface alters the relationship between approach velocity, contact compliance, and peak impact force. The calculation below models this interaction using an undamped kinetic-to-strain energy balance, demonstrating how penalty spring compliance distorts the resulting force profile.

The manipulator drives the latch tool into the strike plate with an effective moving mass of 9 kg at the tool center point. This value is a stipulation, not a derivation. The registry takes the mass an arm presents at its tool as half its structural mass, the same order as the third of its mass that a uniform link pivoting at its base presents at its tip. This scenario drops the 3 kg payload capacity because the gripper holds no external object at impact. In a physical manipulator, reflected inertia varies with arm posture, and drivetrain elasticity partially decouples motor rotor inertia during rapid impact transients (Haddadin et al. 2009). Consequently, effective impact mass is an empirical parameter that physical paired probing must calibrate. Equating initial kinetic energy to elastic strain energy at maximum deflection, \(\tfrac{1}{2} \Mass \SpeedAtDetection^2 = \UnbrakedImpactForce^2 / (2\ContactStiffness)\), yields the peak impact force for an unpowered mass \(\Mass\) impacting a linear spring of stiffness \(\ContactStiffness\) at velocity \(\SpeedAtDetection\): \[\UnbrakedImpactForce = \SpeedAtDetection\sqrt{\ContactStiffness\,\Mass}. \tag{7}\] On physical hardware, the compliance does not reside solely in the strike plate. The robot’s rubber finger pads, mechanical linkages, and elastic joints sit directly between the effective link mass and the strike plate, acting as a series spring chain. Because the contact force transmits equally through each element in series, total mechanical deflection is additive: \(\FiniteChange \ContactNormalDeflection = \NormalContactForce/\LatchPlateStiffness + \NormalContactForce/\RobotStructuralStiffness\). The coupled assembly responds with an effective series stiffness: \[\EffectiveSeriesStiffness = \left(\frac{1}{\LatchPlateStiffness} + \frac{1}{\RobotStructuralStiffness}\right)^{-1}. \tag{8}\] In the limit of an infinitely rigid manipulator (\(\RobotStructuralStiffness \to \infty\)), the effective stiffness collapses to \(\EffectiveSeriesStiffness = \LatchPlateStiffness\), establishing an upper bound on impact force. In contrast, the penalty simulator implements a standalone compliant spring, whose artificial stiffness depends entirely on the maximum allowable penetration depth.

Haddadin, Sami, Alin Albu-Schäffer, and Gerd Hirzinger. 2009. “Requirements for Safe Robots: Measurements, Analysis and New Insights.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 28 (11–12): 1507–27. https://doi.org/10.1177/0278364909343970.

Napkin Math 6.3: The soft-spring exploit
Problem: The simulator’s penalty contact lets the gripper sink 2.4 mm into the plate at the fast approach. What peak force does the modeled contact produce, what peak can the plate impose in the rigid-arm limit, and at what robot-side stiffness does the passive model cross the latch force limit?

Variables:

  • Moving mass \(\Mass =\) 9 kg (stipulated from the arm’s datasheet mass)
  • Fast approach \(\SpeedAtDetection =\) 0.10 m/s and guarded approach 0.03 m/s (illustrative)
  • Penalty penetration \(\PenaltyPenetrationDepth =\) 2.4 mm at the fast approach (illustrative)
  • Plate stiffness \(\LatchPlateStiffness =\) 4.0 × 10⁵ N/m and latch limit \(\LatchForceLimit =\) 100 N (illustrative)

Math:

  1. Simulated spring: The penetration fixes \(\PenaltyContactStiffness = \Mass \SpeedAtDetection^2/\PenaltyPenetrationDepth^2 =\) 1.6 × 10⁴ N/m, so equation 7 gives \(\SimulatedUnbrakedImpactForce = \Mass \SpeedAtDetection^2/\PenaltyPenetrationDepth =\) 9 kg \(\times\) (0.10 m/s)\(^2 /\) 2.4 mm \(=\) 37.5 N, under the limit.
  2. Rigid robot: With \(\ContactStiffness = \LatchPlateStiffness\), \(\UnbrakedImpactForce = \SpeedAtDetection\sqrt{\ContactStiffness\,\Mass} =\) 0.10 m/s \(\times\) (4.0 × 10⁵ N/m \(\times\) 9 kg)\(^{1/2}\) \(=\) 189.7 N, 1.9× the limit.
  3. Break-even chain: The limit is reached at \(\EffectiveSeriesStiffness^* = (\LatchForceLimit/\SpeedAtDetection)^2/\Mass =\) 1.11 × 10⁵ N/m, which equation 8 turns into \(\RobotStructuralStiffness^* = (1/\EffectiveSeriesStiffness^* - 1/\LatchPlateStiffness)^{-1} =\) 1.54 × 10⁵ N/m.
  4. Guarded approach: A rigid robot coasting in at 0.03 m/s peaks at 56.9 N.

Result: In this passive impact model, the fast approach exceeds the force limit if the robot side is stiffer than 1.54 × 10⁵ N/m and remains below it if softer; the guarded approach remains below the limit even with a rigid robot.

Systems insight: A spring 25.6× softer than the plate placed the training result on the safe side of a threshold that the machine’s own compliance decides. No training score reveals whether the physical robot sits above or below \(\RobotStructuralStiffness^*\); empirical hardware measurement provides the only valid ground truth.

The teacher’s reward kept only the task and time terms of equation 1, and the simulated contact placed the plunge on the compliant side of the damage boundary, so the plunge cost the optimizer nothing, shortened every episode, and earned maximum reward. (Had the force term been present, the simulated 37.5 N peak would have crossed the 15 N tripwire and drawn a penalty, but the real plate’s peak would still have gone unseen.) Consequently, the policy converged on an aggressive, unbraked plunge. In the absence of an empirical measurement of \(\RobotStructuralStiffness\) for this specific arm, gripper, and fingertip assembly, the actual physical outcome remains undetermined. Determining true system behavior requires conducting a quasi-static force-deflection test at the tool center point. Until physical characterization confirms whether effective stiffness falls below \(\RobotStructuralStiffness^*\), accepting the policy’s simulated success as proof of safety is unwarranted.

Past about 1.54 × 10⁵ N/m of arm stiffness, the passive model predicts a force peak above the latch limit.

While the energy balance model analyzes an unpowered mass coasting into an obstacle, an actively powered drive pushing forward represents the failure mode that safety supervisory logic must interrupt. Contact force follows Hooke’s law, \(\NormalContactForce = \ContactStiffness\,\FiniteChange \ContactNormalDeflection\). Assuming the actuator drives the mechanism into the plate at constant velocity \(\SpeedAtDetection\), penetration depth scales as \(\FiniteChange \ContactNormalDeflection = \SpeedAtDetection \TimeInstant\), driving a linear force ramp \(\NormalContactForce(\TimeInstant) = \ContactStiffness \SpeedAtDetection \TimeInstant\) starting from the instant of physical contact. Setting \(\NormalContactForce(\TimeInstant) = \LatchForceLimit\) defines the contact yield deadline: \[\ContactYieldDeadline = \frac{\LatchForceLimit}{\ContactStiffness \SpeedAtDetection}, \tag{9}\] representing the available time before exceeding the mechanical structural threshold under constant-velocity, linear-elastic conditions. (A complete stopping analysis must additionally account for controller braking trajectories and actuator force slew rates.) Assuming an infinitely rigid manipulator yields a conservative lower bound on this deadline by using the strike plate stiffness \(\LatchPlateStiffness\) directly.

Under these conditions, a fast approach at 0.10 m/s reaches the latch limit of 100 N in 100 N \(/\) (4.0 × 10⁵ N/m \(\times\) 0.10 m/s) \(=\) 2.5 ms after touch. The 15 N tripwire fires at 0.375 ms. Sampling, intervention computation, and drive response then consume 2 ms, placing brake onset at 2.375 ms. Only 0.125 ms remains before the counterfactual constant-drive ramp reaches the force limit. At the guarded approach of 0.03 m/s, the tripwire fires at 1.25 ms and braking begins at 3.25 ms, leaving 5.083 ms before the 8.333 ms touch-relative limit (figure 6). Neither remaining interval establishes that the actual braking trajectory stays below the limit.

The plate-only deadline leaves too little demonstrated braking margin to credit the reactive force monitor at the fast approach, so protection must act before contact until the contact response is qualified. An independently monitored deceleration envelope, \(\SafeApproachSpeedEnvelope(\StoppingBoundaryClearance) \le \sqrt{2 \CredibleBrakingDeceleration \StoppingBoundaryClearance}\), is the stopping inequality of Delay consumes stopping clearance applied to the gripper. Here \(\StoppingBoundaryClearance\) is the clearance to a stopping boundary placed a short standoff before the plate, after sensing and actuation latencies are charged, and \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) is the credible deceleration of the arm at the tool, not the base’s value on the machine card. Because \(\SafeApproachSpeedEnvelope\) falls to zero at that boundary, the envelope forbids an unbraked arrival at the plate. Crossing the standoff is a separate act. A guarded contact mode, qualified on its own, takes over inside it and holds the gripper at or below the guarded approach speed of 0.03 m/s until touch. That mode needs its own evidence about approach speed, force rise rate, loop latency, and deceleration, and a force tripwire works only inside those validated bounds. This contact deadline, not the reward used in training, is what confines the policy’s declared ODD to the guarded approach speed (section 6.8).

Engineering teams must budget contact simulation fidelity in terms of observable physical quantities, such as contact onset timing, peak normal force, total transferred impulse, slip distance, and separation release delay, rather than abstract solver convergence metrics. Discrepancies between simulation and reality frequently manifest in both directions. For example, a penalty solver that resolves an impact within a single integration step concentrates momentum transfer into an impulse spike of extreme amplitude and sub-millisecond duration. On real hardware, viscoelastic fingertip deformation and joint compliance distribute that momentum transfer over dozens of milliseconds. In such cases, total integrated impulse may match closely between simulation and reality even as peak force differs by an order of magnitude. A policy trained to trigger reflexive retraction upon sensing an impulse spike will fail on the physical robot, because the attenuated real-world signal never crosses the detection threshold, causing the motor drive to stall against its maximum current limit.

⇄ Contrast: The 2.4 mm of penalty-spring penetration is compliance the solver invents, whereas the backlash and torsional compliance of the real drivetrain, analyzed in Transmission compliance, contact impedance, and tooth fracture, are compliance a rigid-body solver omits.

Systems Perspective 6.1: The contact fidelity invariant
Never trust a simulated contact policy whose success depends on instantaneous momentum absorption or zero-delay friction engagement; a policy that relies on penetrating the digital surface loads the physical workpiece with force the simulator never showed.

The operational relevance of specific physical omissions depends directly on the task dynamics. A policy executing uninterrupted, high-velocity motions may tolerate crude Coulomb friction models because joint velocities never linger near zero. Conversely, a policy tasked with precise force regulation or static holding will destabilize into violent limit-cycle oscillations if the simulator ignores static friction thresholds, stick-slip transitions, and actuator deadbands.

Validating simulation fidelity requires paired probing. The systems engineer executes identical open-loop actuation trajectories on both the physical robot and its digital twin, quantifying trajectory divergence against explicit error budgets. For the latch assembly, this procedure commands identical velocity profiles into the simulated and physical strike plates, measuring discrepancies directly in milliseconds and newtons. Whenever empirical divergence violates the error margins demanded by the robot’s operating envelope, the simulator cannot be trusted to train that specific capability. That audit relies on knowing which differential equations the simulator solved; when the simulator is itself a neural network trained on video or trajectory logs, even the list of omitted mechanisms disappears.

6.4.3 Learned world models as simulators

A policy trained or evaluated inside a learned world model can score near-perfect task completion by exploiting hallucinated physics where training data were sparse. An example is pushing through a contact transition that the neural predictor smooths over because it rarely saw high-force impacts, with no analytical equation available for an engineer to audit. Whereas physics-based simulators are built from explicit differential equations and mechanical models whose simplifications can be enumerated, a world model derives system dynamics from data by training a neural network to predict future observations (or compact latent states) conditioned on the current state and applied control actions. Contemporary physical AI architectures employ learned world models across three distinct operational roles:

  1. Latent imagination: Policies train via reinforcement learning within the simulated latent representations of a world model. For example, DayDreamer and DreamerV3 trained quadruped locomotion directly from physical robot interaction, mastering rolling, standing, and walking behaviors in several hours without hand-crafted physics simulators (Wu et al. 2023; Hafner et al. 2025).
  2. Zero-shot planning: Action-conditioned video models plan trajectory sequences directly. For instance, V-JEPA 2, post-trained on uncurated robotic manipulation video, guided pick-and-place manipulation across physical robot arms in novel laboratory environments without prior domain calibration (Assran et al. 2025).
  3. Synthetic data generation and evaluation: Scaled generative video models synthesize diverse visual trajectories for offline imitation learning and act as simulated environments to evaluate candidate policies (NVIDIA, Agarwal, et al. 2025).
Wu, Philipp, Alejandro Escontrela, Danijar Hafner, Pieter Abbeel, and Ken Goldberg. 2023. “DayDreamer: World Models for Physical Robot Learning.” Proceedings of the 6th Conference on Robot Learning, Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 205: 2226–40.

Unlike a physics engine, a neural world model provides no explicit list of the phenomena it leaves out. Prediction errors concentrate wherever training distribution coverage is sparse, and no analytical derivation exists to reveal which dynamic constraints are violated. Consequently, training data generated purely through world model rollouts must be cataloged as unqualified in the policy manifest, whose simulation_provenance field captures the model architecture, parameter checkpoint hash, and dataset lineage. Simulated success inside a learned world model cannot expand the policy’s declared ODD until empirical paired probing on physical hardware verifies the specific contact forces, transport latencies, and tracking tolerances required by the task. Treating a world model’s internal rollout predictions as verified evidence mistakes model hallucination for physical reality.

Because structural simulation assumptions, numerical integrator time steps, and contact approximations become encoded in the policy’s weights during training, the simulation configuration is an immutable part of the model’s lineage. Every deployable policy artifact must trace its complete provenance through the simulation_provenance metadata block (section 6.8). Deploying policy weights without explicit simulator provenance severs the trained model from the kinematic limits, noise distributions, and solver thresholds under which it was derived. A scalar task completion rate cannot audit that lineage. The reality gap has to be taken apart into its dynamics, sensing, appearance, and timing channels, each measured on its own.

6.5 Physical Channels of the Sim-to-Real Gap

A robotic simulation rarely diverges from physical hardware in only one channel. In the cage-door latch mechanism, for instance, the simulator departs from reality in two different ways: a penalty spring far softer than the physical strike plate, and an idealized command path that omits transport lag. Collapsing these discrepancies into a single scalar trajectory error blends them into an uninformative aggregate that obscures both mechanisms. Errors in different physical channels carry different units, propagate along distinct paths within the closed loop, and trigger different failure modes. The sim-to-real mismatch must therefore be treated as a coupled vector of physical components spanning dynamics, sensing, appearance, and timing. Sizing this vector requires an explicit accounting of physical errors against task tolerances, evaluated from paired command-response traces recorded under identical excitations. Each channel demands its own mitigation and runtime interceptor. Table 2 lists these interceptors across actuator dynamics, contact mechanics, sensing, and timing; appearance mismatch is sized separately and charted in panel (d) of figure 7.

Definition 6.2: Sim-to-real reality gap vector

Sim-to-real reality gap vector \(\RealityGapVector = [\DynamicsRealityGap, \SensingRealityGap, \AppearanceRealityGap, \TimingRealityGap]\) is the coupled, multi-channel decomposition of physical discrepancies between simulated and hardware environments spanning contact mechanics (\(\DynamicsRealityGap\)), sensor transduction and noise (\(\SensingRealityGap\)), visual appearance (\(\AppearanceRealityGap\)), and asynchronous transport latency and jitter (\(\TimingRealityGap\)).

  1. Significance: Treating the “reality gap” as a scalar difficulty metric or pure visual domain mismatch causes engineers to overlook dynamical and temporal divergences. Discrepancies in actuator back-EMF, gearbox compliance, or bus jitter deplete feedback stability margins before the policy completes its first physical control step.
  2. Distinction: An inaccurate parameter value (such as a 10 percent error in link mass) preserves the causal differential equations of motion, whereas an omitted mechanism (such as zero-delay communication or unmodeled Coulomb stick-slip) entirely severs physical feedback paths, leading policies to exploit non-physical simulator artifacts.
  3. Common pitfall: Relying on domain randomization alone to bridge a large gap. Randomization covers only the mechanisms the simulator models; for a memoryless policy, indistinguishable parameter instances can favor a compromise or leave low-probability cases poorly served.

Dynamics mismatch measures discrepancies between commanded actuator effort and the resulting physical motion or contact force across the operating envelope. Its contact case is the impulse-spike example of section 6.4.2. A simulator with rigid links, zero backlash, and stiff contact predicts an abrupt force onset. Real drivetrain compliance and drive delays soften and delay that rise. A policy that keyed its braking to the sharp onset keeps driving into the hard stop. The mismatch has the opposite sign of the soft-spring exploit, where the simulator was softer than the real plate rather than stiffer, and the two together are why the dynamics channel has to be measured in both directions.

Sensing mismatch measures discrepancies between the true physical state of the machine and the observation vector delivered to the policy network. Simulated range and depth sensors typically yield clean, uniformly quantized measurements corrupted only by independent zero-mean Gaussian noise. Physical sensors, by contrast, suffer from optical calibration bias, structural noise, temperature-dependent drift, and sustained dropout bursts caused by specular reflections on polished surfaces. An uncalibrated range bias shifts the physical standoff distance where the controller transitions from rapid approach to gentle force regulation. A multi-frame dropout burst leaves the policy momentarily blind during high-speed descent, creating an unobserved window where collisions occur before feedback can intervene.

Image-space distance metrics such as Mean Squared Error (MSE), Peak Signal-to-Noise Ratio (PSNR), or Fréchet Inception Distance (FID) cannot reliably qualify appearance transfer. These summary statistics fail to weight visual discrepancies by policy sensitivity, that is, by the policy Jacobian \(\partial \CognitivePolicyAction / \partial \MultimodalObservation\) relating actions to observations. Consequently, a benign aggregate difference can mask a task-critical localization error. A photorealistic rendering with ray-traced lighting might register a high pixel MSE because of background shadows or wall textures that the policy’s visual encoder discards. Conversely, a sub-millimeter pixel shift along the silhouette of an assembly peg might alter the overall image MSE by less than 0.1 percent. Yet that subtle shift moves the policy’s predicted insertion coordinate outside the mechanical chamfer tolerance, driving the peg directly into a rigid fixture shoulder. Appearance mismatch must therefore be quantified by evaluating policy-relevant perceptual representations (detected keypoints, surface normal tokens, or latent feature centroids) under controlled variations in physical geometry, illumination, material reflectance, and camera exposure.

Timing mismatch governs the temporal alignment between observation capture, policy evaluation, and actuation. Identical state transitions occurring at different moments in physical time yield entirely different closed-loop dynamics. A simulation that executes observation, inference, and actuation instantaneously at the beginning of each discrete integration step omits camera sensor exposure times, PCIe bus serialization, neural network tail latency, and EtherCAT fieldbus transport, along with their cycle-to-cycle jitter. Every millisecond of omitted transport delay translates directly into physical distance the mechanism traverses open-loop before corrective actuation begins. Unmodeled delay also introduces frequency-dependent phase lag. Near gain crossover, the added lag consumes the available open-loop phase margin. Whether the loop remains stable depends on the sampled plant, compensation, jitter, and total response; Discrete-time sampling and zero-order hold phase lag quantifies the delay contribution.

A single illustrative scenario, kept separate from the latch so that its numbers are not mistaken for latch measurements, shows the four channels diverging at once (figure 7). A linear drive approaches a surface at 0.20 m/s and regulates contact force to 10 N. In the dynamics channel, an unmodeled drive delay of 8 ms and a softer physical contact pad cut the force rise from 500 N/s in simulation to 250 N/s on hardware (a rate the force regulator sets as it ramps toward its target, far below the free-impact rate \(\ContactStiffness \SpeedAtDetection\) of the latch analysis). The target force therefore arrives at 40 ms instead of 20 ms. The drive overtravels by 3 mm, and a hard current-limit shutdown trips. In the sensing channel, a calibration bias of +3.2 mm triggers premature deceleration, and a specular dropout burst of 4 frames blinds the controller during final descent. In appearance, a pixel difference of only 5.5 percent hides a 6 mm lateral shift in the contact-patch estimate, more than the 2 mm chamfer tolerance. In timing, an illustrative p99 actuation delay of 49.5 ms carries the mechanism 9.9 mm past the point the simulator predicts with its idealized 0 ms observation-to-actuation delay. The simulator’s 20 ms integration step is a numerical parameter, not pipeline latency, and cannot be subtracted from the physical transport delay. Each channel fails in its own units (milliseconds of force rise, millimeters of bias, millimeters of patch shift, millimeters of unobserved travel). A scalar loss can report that hardware departs from simulation but cannot say which channel failed, and separating the channels is what lets table 2 give each one its own paired-trace acceptance test and its own permission-path interceptor.

The first line of defense against dynamics mismatch is empirical measurement. System identification9 calibrates the simulator’s parameter values (link masses, friction coefficients, torque constants, and transport delays) against paired command-response traces recorded on the physical robot. When a physical subsystem resists accurate first-principles modeling, engineers can replace analytical equations with data-driven approximations. In quadrupedal locomotion work, Hwangbo et al. (2019) trained an actuator network that predicted physical joint torque directly from a rolling history of position errors and joint velocities, executing this neural network inside the simulation loop instead of an idealized torque actuator. Similarly, delta-action residual models learn state-dependent corrections from physical rollouts, adjusting simulated commands so that simulated trajectories track physical behavior (He et al. 2025). In the cage-door latch task, these techniques help reconcile actuator response and bus transport latency, yet they share one limit. A learned correction model is valid only across the empirical state distribution visited during data collection, and outside that distribution the model is uncharacterized. Consequently, any learned simulator correction must enter the policy manifest’s omission log with its own explicitly bounded domain of validity.

9 Identification Experiments: System identification treats the input excitation signal as part of the experiment design. Signals such as step functions, swept-frequency sines (chirps), and pseudo-random binary sequences (PRBS) must persistently excite the relevant mechanical and electrical modes, since nominal closed-loop operation rarely activates them (Ljung 1999). Paired physical probing follows this discipline: it drives both the simulator and physical hardware with identical open-loop excitation trajectories rather than relying on passive operational logs, where feedback regulation actively keeps the machine away from the boundary contact dynamics listed in the omission log.

Ljung, Lennart. 1999. System Identification: Theory for the User. 2nd ed. Prentice Hall.
He, Tairan, Jiawei Gao, Wenli Xiao, Yuanhang Zhang, Zi Wang, Jiashun Wang, Zhengyi Luo, et al. 2025. “ASAP: Aligning Simulation and Real-World Physics for Learning Agile Humanoid Whole-Body Skills.” Robotics: Science and Systems XXI. https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2025.XXI.066.

10 Domain Randomization: The technique originated in computer vision, where Tobin et al. (2017) randomized textures, lighting, and camera poses so that physical scenes appeared as ordinary variations to a detector trained purely on synthetic renders. Peng et al. (2018) extended the concept to dynamics, randomizing link masses, friction coefficients, joint damping, and transport latencies for robotic manipulation. Appearance and dynamics differ in how a policy can exploit history. Appearance variation changes instantaneous visual observations, whereas dynamics variation changes how the physical plant responds to control inputs, a discrepancy that a policy with temporal memory can infer online.

Tobin, Josh, Rachel Fong, Alex Ray, Jonas Schneider, Wojciech Zaremba, and Pieter Abbeel. 2017. “Domain Randomization for Transferring Deep Neural Networks from Simulation to the Real World.” 2017 IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS), 23–30. https://doi.org/10.1109/IROS.2017.8202133.
Peng, Xue Bin, Marcin Andrychowicz, Wojciech Zaremba, and Pieter Abbeel. 2018. “Sim-to-Real Transfer of Robotic Control with Dynamics Randomization.” 2018 IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 3803–10. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA.2018.8460528.

Repeatedly training against a single point estimate of physical parameters, such as an assumed strike-plate stiffness, encourages a policy to overfit to that exact numerical value. Domain randomization10 addresses parameter uncertainty by perturbing modeled simulation parameters across plausible distributions during training, forcing the policy to encounter varied physical responses (Tobin et al. 2017; Peng et al. 2018). However, sampling from a parameter distribution merely defines training weights; it does not constitute a formal certificate of robustness across the parameter set. How effectively a policy handles this diversity depends heavily on whether its architecture incorporates memory.

In standard reinforcement learning, a policy maximizes expected return within a single nominal simulation environment parameterized by the static vector \(\SimulationParameterVector_0\): \[\ExpectedReturnObjective(\ModelParameters; \SimulationParameterVector_0) = \mathbb{E}_{\RolloutStateSequence, \RolloutActionSequence \sim \PolicyTrajectoryDistribution(\cdot \mid \SimulationParameterVector_0)} \left[ \sum_{k=0}^\Time \DiscountFactor^\RolloutStepIndex \Reward(\PhysicalWorldState_{k}, \CognitivePolicyAction_{k}) \right].\] Domain randomization reformulates the physical environment as a distribution over plausible Markov Decision Processes parameterized by physical vector \(\SimulationParameterVector \sim \ProbabilityFunction(\SimulationParameterVector)\), where \(\SimulationParameterVector\) encompasses link masses, centers of mass, surface friction coefficients, joint damping, transport delays, and sensor noise variances. The domain randomization objective maximizes the expected return marginalized across this distribution: \[\DomainRandomizationObjective(\ModelParameters) = \mathbb{E}_{\SimulationParameterVector \sim \ProbabilityFunction(\SimulationParameterVector)} \left[ \ExpectedReturnObjective(\ModelParameters; \SimulationParameterVector) \right] = \int_{\SimulationParameterSet} \left( \mathbb{E}_{\RolloutStateSequence, \RolloutActionSequence \sim \PolicyTrajectoryDistribution(\cdot \mid \SimulationParameterVector)} \left[ \sum_{k=0}^\Time \DiscountFactor^\RolloutStepIndex \Reward(\PhysicalWorldState_{k}, \CognitivePolicyAction_{k}) \right] \right) \ProbabilityFunction(\SimulationParameterVector)\,\DerivativeOperator\SimulationParameterVector. \tag{10}\] Marginalizing over \(\ProbabilityFunction(\SimulationParameterVector)\) incentivizes policies to maintain performance across sampled physical configurations rather than collapsing to a single operating point. Nevertheless, optimizing expected return can mask catastrophic control failures occurring in low-probability regions of the distribution; high average reward does not imply uniform stability across the support \(\SimulationParameterSet\).

An alternative formulation adopts a minimax objective from robust control, optimizing performance against the worst-case modeled configuration: \[\max_\ModelParameters \min_{\SimulationParameterVector \in \SimulationParameterSet} \ExpectedReturnObjective(\ModelParameters; \SimulationParameterVector).\] Here, the inner minimization identifies the worst-case return within the bounded uncertainty set \(\SimulationParameterSet\), while the outer optimization searches for policy parameters \(\ModelParameters\) that maximize this floor. Minimax optimization targets the worst return within the modeled set, but this remains an optimization statement over modeled rewards rather than a physical stability proof. Neither distribution-averaging nor minimax optimization can protect against physical mechanisms that the simulator omits.

Two constraints govern domain randomization. First, hardware operating outside the sampled support (\(\HardwareParameterVector \notin \text{supp}(\ProbabilityFunction(\SimulationParameterVector))\)) is entirely unrepresented during training. Second, parameter randomization cannot compensate for structural omissions in the physics engine: no amount of variation in link mass or friction will expose a policy to the unmodeled lost motion of a harmonic drive or the preemptive jitter of a real-time operating system. Within the modeled support, the operational effect of broad randomization depends on whether the policy maintains temporal state. A memoryless feedforward network receives only the current observation \(\MultimodalObservation_{t}\). When different parameter instances produce indistinguishable observations but require different actions, the policy cannot adapt its choice to the active instance. Optimizing average return may then favor a compromise, and neither conservatism nor stability across the envelope is guaranteed. Conversely, a policy that conditions on a temporal history of observations and actions can infer physical plant dynamics online from the system’s transient response, effectively performing implicit system identification. For this reason, recurrent policies trained across randomized dynamics transfer with substantially higher fidelity than memoryless architectures (Peng et al. 2018), and memory is what lets a wider randomization range produce adaptation to the real plant rather than conservative detuning.

In engineering practice, parameter distributions are configured through two distinct strategies grounded in different forms of evidence. In calibration-first engineering, developers measure nominal masses, torque constants, bus latencies, and friction parameters directly on test benches. Randomization bounds are then derived from known physical mechanisms. For example, motor winding thermal limits determine the envelope of torque-constant variation, and surface degradation models govern friction bounds. These ranges represent verifiable physical claims about the robot and its environment. In contrast, automatic domain randomization (ADR) initializes the simulator at calibrated values and dynamically expands parameter ranges as the policy’s training performance at the distribution boundaries exceeds predefined success thresholds (OpenAI et al. 2019). ADR parameter bounds reflect the limits of what the reinforcement learning optimization can master rather than physical properties of the hardware, often expanding to unphysical extremes. The dexterity achieved by ADR in robotic in-hand manipulation relied on a recurrent policy learning to adapt online to these artificially broad dynamics. Regardless of the scheduling strategy, paired hardware traces must ultimately audit each channel against explicit error budgets before deployment; discrepancies exceeding tolerance require simulator revision rather than blind parameter widening. A randomization range expanded by an automated training curriculum represents an optimization scaffold, not an empirical characterization of the hardware, and therefore cannot expand the declared ODD.

Where domain randomization and simulation fidelity reach their physical limits, deterministic safety mechanisms must enforce system invariants. Every action proposed by a learned policy must pass through an independent permission path before reaching hardware actuators. Within this execution layer, hard force-derivative clamps, torque slew-rate limiters, and velocity-dependent stopping governors restrict commands to the envelope supported by verified sensing and braking capabilities.

↪ The Control Barrier Function filter that minimally corrects unverified policy proposals on every permission tick is developed in The Minimal Intervention Principle and Optimization Filters.

Decomposing the reality gap channel by channel divides the operating space in two. Inside the region where paired traces match simulation within verified tolerances, the simulated experience counts as support. Every unmodeled phenomenon, uncalibrated parameter, and unrepresented disturbance outside it becomes an entry in the omission log, the policy manifest’s record of what the simulator leaves out (section 6.8). The log therefore holds two kinds of entry, mechanisms the simulator omits and parameters no paired probe has yet calibrated, because an uncalibrated parameter leaves the simulated experience just as unsupported until a probe bounds it. For the latch, one of each heads the log: the strike-plate stiffness, a parameter the penalty spring sets far softer than the plate, and the transport latency of the actuator bus, a mechanism the simulator omits. No paired probe has yet closed either, so the high-speed contact states they govern fall outside what the simulation supports. Each entry also states a requirement for a runtime monitor or safety filter, which must detect when execution leaves the regime where the policy’s simulated training remains valid. Calibrating or randomizing simulator parameters still leaves a second gap: the simulator exposes exact contact forces, friction coefficients, and fixture offsets during training that the physical robot’s onboard sensors cannot directly measure at run time.

6.5.1 Teacher-student privileged state distillation and asymmetric actor-critic

While policy training can exploit privileged simulator state, such as ground-truth contact wrenches, friction coefficients, and exact strike-plate offsets, the onboard policy proposer must execute exclusively from the noisy, bandwidth-limited sensors installed on the physical machine. In actor-critic reinforcement learning, the actor proposes control actions while the critic evaluates expected future returns to compute policy gradients. Systems engineers bridge this sensor gap through two main architectures. Asymmetric actor-critic grants privileged observations exclusively to the critic during training. Teacher-student distillation first trains a privileged teacher policy and subsequently trains a sensor-limited student policy to imitate the teacher’s actions. Both architectures require validating whether the runtime observation stream delivered to the student contains enough information to reconstruct or compensate for the missing state.

The core challenge stems from partial observability, which casts physical robot control as a partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP).11 Identical sequences of measured joint positions and velocities can produce drastically different physical trajectories depending on whether a footpad grips dry concrete or slips on wet oil. A conventional critic \(\CriticReturnEstimate(\MultimodalObservation_{t})\) conditioned solely on instantaneous sensory observations cannot distinguish between these physical regimes, producing high-variance or biased value estimates. In simulation, exposing hidden contact wrenches \(\ContactWrench\), friction coefficients \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\), terrain profiles, and payload inertias directly to the critic stabilizes temporal-difference learning without burdening the onboard runtime actor.

11 Partially Observable MDP: Åström (1965) formulated optimal control under incomplete state information, proving that an optimal controller must act on the conditional probability distribution of the state given the observation history (the belief state) rather than solely on the instantaneous observation. Reinforcement learning formalized this framework three decades later (Kaelbling et al. 1998). An onboard student network’s temporal observation history serves as a learned approximation of this belief state; its temporal window must span sufficient duration for physical contact and load dynamics to become observable in the sensor stream.

Åström, Karl Johan. 1965. “Optimal Control of Markov Processes with Incomplete State Information.” Journal of Mathematical Analysis and Applications 10 (1): 174–205. https://doi.org/10.1016/0022-247X(65)90154-X.
Kaelbling, Leslie Pack, Michael L. Littman, and Anthony R. Cassandra. 1998. “Planning and Acting in Partially Observable Stochastic Domains.” Artificial Intelligence 101 (1–2): 99–134. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0004-3702(98)00023-X.
Pinto, Lerrel, Marcin Andrychowicz, Peter Welinder, Wojciech Zaremba, and Pieter Abbeel. 2018. “Asymmetric Actor Critic for Image-Based Robot Learning.” Robotics: Science and Systems XIV. https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2018.XIV.008.
Lee, Joonho, Jemin Hwangbo, Lorenz Wellhausen, Vladlen Koltun, and Marco Hutter. 2020. “Learning Quadrupedal Locomotion over Challenging Terrain.” Science Robotics 5 (47): eabc5986.
Kumar, Ashish, Zipeng Fu, Deepak Pathak, and Jitendra Malik. 2021. “RMA: Rapid Motor Adaptation for Legged Robots.” Robotics: Science and Systems XVII. https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2021.XVII.011.

Asymmetric actor-critic and teacher-student distillation deploy privileged information through contrasting computational pipelines (Pinto et al. 2018; Lee et al. 2020; Kumar et al. 2021). Under asymmetric actor-critic, the actor receives only the onboard sensor history available at runtime, while the critic receives full privileged simulation state. Because the critic is discarded after training, the deployed actor architecture remains clean and lightweight. However, training an unprivileged actor directly against complex, contact-rich tasks can suffer from high sample complexity.

Distillation splits the work across the proposal boundary (figure 8). Everything privileged stays on the simulation side, and only the frozen student’s proposals, built from the machine’s own sensor history and renewed every 50 ms, cross to the permission path. In Phase 1, the privileged teacher policy \(\pi_{\text{teacher}}(\CognitivePolicyAction_{t} \mid \PrivilegedSimulatorState, \MultimodalObservation_{t})\) learns an effective control strategy via standard reinforcement learning, exploiting full access to contact forces and surface friction. In Phase 2, the student policy \(\pi_{\text{student}}(\CognitivePolicyAction_{t} \mid \ObservationHistoryWindow)\) is trained via supervised imitation learning (such as DAgger or behavioral cloning) to reproduce the teacher’s proposed actions using only a sliding history of onboard observations. While this two-phase approach simplifies reinforcement learning exploration, it introduces a transfer bottleneck. If the teacher’s control strategy relies on distinctions that cannot be reconstructed from the onboard observation history \(\ObservationHistoryWindow\), the student cannot replicate the teacher’s behavior. Privileged information accelerates training convergence, but it cannot make physically unobservable states identifiable on hardware.

Consider the cage-door latch mechanism. A student network equipped with a temporal history can compare commanded motor efforts against observed joint accelerations over recent timesteps, inferring changes in contact stiffness or friction through the resulting mechanical resistance. However, identical joint kinematics can arise from multiple distinct physical causes, and an unobserved geometric offset in the strike plate cannot be inferred simply because the simulation teacher possessed that knowledge. A temporal convolutional network or transformer encoder can output a learned latent embedding of the environment, but its accuracy must be audited against physical ground truth. Adding auxiliary loss functions to predict privileged parameters during distillation regularizes the representation, but an auxiliary loss cannot create physical information that is absent from the underlying sensor stream.

Selecting the observation history length \(\ObservationHistoryLength\) (distinct from the chunk horizon \(\ActionChunkHorizon\)) involves a trade-off in real-time estimator design. A window that is too short cannot filter high-frequency sensor noise or integrate enough kinematic evidence to distinguish sudden payload changes from transient disturbances. Conversely, an excessively long window increases onboard memory footprint, inflates neural inference latency, and risks diluting transient signals, such as the exact moment of initial mechanical contact, under an accumulation of pre-contact free-space observations. System qualification for the cage-door latch policy must verify contact-onset braking dynamics under the chosen history length while accounting for real hardware sensing delays. Buffering a longer temporal history in memory does not inherently add latency to incoming sensor frames, but the temporal encoder must be structured so that the newest observation immediately influences the output action.

Checkpoint 6.2: Simulator fidelity and transfer

A simulator supplies cheap experience only for the mechanisms it models, and the evidence that it models them comes from the machine.

Sizing the gap

Crediting the result

Even after system identification, domain randomization, and privileged state distillation, residual discrepancies persist in high-precision, contact-rich operational regimes. For the latch, both omission-log entries remain open. Engineers can resolve certain localized kinematic and friction mismatches through fine-tuning directly on the physical fixture. Physical training, however, consumes hardware operational life through mechanical wear and reopens qualification claims that depend on changed proposer behavior, timing, interfaces, or physical conditions. Every updated policy checkpoint produced by on-robot training demands fresh qualification evidence before it can be cleared for autonomous operation.

6.6 Real-Robot Fine-Tuning and Residual Control

Fine-tuning a simulation-trained policy directly on a physical robot to absorb a small fixture offset can fix the local contact error while overheating the motor windings, spending gearbox fatigue life on exploratory impacts, and silently corrupting the free-space approach trajectories that had already passed qualification. Mechanical assemblies rarely match their models exactly: on a warehouse cage door, for example, the physical strike plate may sit at a small mounting offset relative to its nominal position in the simulation model. By computing gradient updates directly from physical rollouts, on-robot fine-tuning adapts policy parameters to the true structural compliance, contact dynamics, and bus latencies of the hardware. However, because end-to-end policy gradients optimize cumulative task return rather than identifying individual system parameters, they do not isolate or quantify specific physical mechanisms. Unmodeled simulation discrepancies, such as an idealized contact spring or an omitted communication transport lag, therefore remain open in the engineering omission log until dedicated system-identification probes characterize them.

Furthermore, physical adaptation exacts a concrete hardware toll. In simulation, sampling aggressive state transitions near the boundaries of stability costs only compute cycles. On physical hardware, the exploratory actions and gradient updates that correct a contact error consume mechanical fatigue life and generate substantial heat in the motor windings. Every exploratory rollout risks driving the mechanism into an unrecoverable contact jam or an over-current trip, and unconstrained parameter updates can silently degrade nominal, previously certified behaviors.

Consider adapting the baseline policy latch-sim-02 into latch-res-03, a residual candidate that adds a gated learned correction to the frozen latch-sim-02 setpoints. This adaptation is motivated by the physical reality of the hardware fixture: the physical strike plate sits 1.5 mm away from its modeled location in simulation. The physical mechanism must guide a spring-loaded latch past this plate, where non-smooth Coulomb friction, cam surface geometry, and spring preloads produce sharp stick-slip transitions. The policy outputs setpoints every 25 ms (40 Hz). To adapt its control commands to the strike plate’s contact dynamics, an on-robot fine-tuning routine collects 20,000 interaction steps, totaling 500 s of active contact and near-contact operation.

During exploration, stochastic perturbations can inject force transients faster than the low-level controller can reject them. If 1.5 percent of the sampled steps breach the 15 N contact tripwire, the fine-tuning run records 300 above-threshold samples. However, engineers must not mistake these raw sample counts for independent mechanical collisions. Consecutive over-threshold steps typically belong to a single sustained contact episode rather than multiple distinct impacts. Accurately counting mechanical shocks and evaluating their damage potential requires temporal event segmentation alongside continuous force-time telemetry.

Every mechanical impact transmits impulsive loads directly into the drivetrain. Translating that cumulative stress into an estimated expenditure of actuator service life requires an empirical, drive-specific load spectrum and fatigue life model. Actuator thermal dynamics impose a second, equally rigid operational boundary. Stochastic exploration dither elevates the root-mean-square current passing through the motor coils. Without explicit cooling intervals scheduled between training episodes, this continuous Joule heating can push motor windings to their thermal derating threshold within minutes.

Demonstrating improved performance on a specific contact fixture does not prove that the overall manipulation task remains sound. An optimization step yielding a parameter update \(\FiniteChange\ModelParameters\) produces an entirely distinct model checkpoint. Empirical evaluations gathered on the prior policy \(\ModelParameters_0\) remain historical observations of \(\ModelParameters_0\) alone; they do not substantiate safety claims for the updated weights. Gradient updates that successfully minimize contact forces on the strike plate can inadvertently distort free-space approach trajectories or degrade emergency stopping profiles in operational regions absent from the fine-tuning rollouts. Because physical trials consume valuable robot runtime and cause mechanical wear, requalification protocols must be strictly prioritized to verify both the targeted contact improvement and the baseline capabilities the update could destabilize.

Each updated checkpoint must carry its own explicit configuration manifest and regression evidence before deployment. Moreover, while optimization progresses on physical hardware, the safety permission path must actively evaluate every candidate action proposed by intermediate policies. Restricting where learned corrections may act keeps unconstrained weight updates from corrupting verified behaviors.

6.6.1 Residual policy architectures and bounded authority

To let latch-res-03 adapt to the strike plate without perturbing its free-space approach, residual architectures freeze the base policy weights and multiply the learned additive correction by an independently enforced binary gate: \[\NominalControlCommand(\DecisionProcessState) = \pi_{\text{base}}(\DecisionProcessState) + \ResidualGate(\DecisionProcessState)\,\FiniteChange\Policy(\DecisionProcessState), \tag{11}\] where \(\pi_{\text{base}}\) is the frozen base controller, \(\FiniteChange\Policy\) is a learned contact correction trained via reinforcement learning on the physical machine against the latch reward of equation 1, and \(\ResidualGate(\DecisionProcessState)\in\{0,1\}\) is an independently evaluated deterministic gate that equals one only within the certified contact envelope.

Both the base policy and the composite controller require explicit verification, because freezing an unverified base policy such as latch-sim-02 does not make it qualified for deployment. Instead, the role of the gate \(\ResidualGate(\DecisionProcessState)\) is structural: it forces the residual correction to evaluate identically to zero in free space and across all non-contact states. There, an absence of reward gradients during training would still allow a function approximator to output nonzero values on out-of-distribution inputs. Before any command reaches physical actuators, the permission path verifies the validity of the current state estimate (the machine’s fused estimate of its own configuration, developed in Spatial Memory) and audits the proposed composite action.

Relying on a soft training loss, such as an action-norm penalty \(\|\FiniteChange\Policy(\DecisionProcessState)\|^2\), merely biases the optimizer toward modest corrections during nominal rollouts; it offers no deterministic runtime guarantee. In contrast, the independent gate explicitly revokes residual authority whenever the robot operates outside designated regimes. Even with an architectural gate, the verification suite must evaluate transient switching behaviors at gate boundaries, state-estimation latency, execution timing jitter, and joint torque limits under the combined controller.

Imposing an output magnitude ceiling, \(\|\ResidualGate(\DecisionProcessState)\FiniteChange\Policy(\DecisionProcessState)\|\le\ResidualCorrectionCeiling\), restricts only one dimension of authority. A production permission path must also enforce slew-rate limits on the correction delta, prevent joint limit violations, verify multi-axis dynamic feasibility, and invoke the qualified fallback when active execution loses valid state, evidence, or lease. A late replacement proposal does not revoke a still-valid active prefix; that prefix may continue only within its own validity conditions. Furthermore, the authorized residual envelope must lie strictly within the verified stability margin of the combined closed-loop system across its intended operating envelope; checking an isolated torque limit against an idealized, single-axis specification is never sufficient. For the latch fixture, for instance, the architecture activates residual corrections exclusively during confirmed physical contact, enforces a hard zero via the gate throughout the free-space approach, and clamps joint torque slew rates. Following each optimization step on hardware, an automated regression suite must re-verify free-space trajectory tracking, contact engagement forces, emergency stopping, thermal duty cycles, and drivetrain shock profiles before granting operational authority to the new checkpoint. An improperly verified residual, even when clipped in magnitude, can still induce limit-cycle oscillations or drive the actuator gearboxes into saturation. Freezing the base policy and gating an additive residual is the most localized way to refine a policy with rewards; other post-training regimes modify the decoder’s input noise or update every weight in the network, widening the regression scope accordingly.

6.6.2 Reinforcement learning after pretraining

The gated residual is one member of a broader family of post-training regimes. Every method that refines a pretrained policy using environmental rewards must specify which parameters are allowed to change, because updating shared weights to double throughput on one fixture can silently degrade baseline behaviors elsewhere. That choice determines how much regression evidence is required to re-qualify the resulting checkpoint. Modern robotic systems use four primary post-training strategies (table 3):

  • Residual control freezes the base policy weights and learns an additive correction term, exactly as demonstrated by latch-res-03. This strategy has been successfully deployed to refine frozen chunked imitation policies for high-precision assembly, training the residual policy via PPO in simulation (Ankile et al. 2025). Residual architectures are straightforward to bound because independent hardware permission logic can directly gate, saturate, and rate-limit the additive output.
  • Noise-space steering freezes every parameter of an underlying diffusion or flow-matching policy, training an auxiliary policy only to predict the initial latent noise vector supplied to the reverse diffusion trajectory. Every proposed trajectory still passes through the frozen generative decoder, so steering is restricted to outputs that decoder can generate. This restriction does not establish that those outputs lie within demonstrated support or satisfy physical limits. Furthermore, the optimizer requires only black-box sampling access to the base policy (Wagenmaker et al. 2025). Here, the support of the base distribution constrains the proposed behaviors; the independent permission path must still determine whether those proposals may execute.
  • Advantage-conditioned post-training updates all network weights using supervised learning conditioned on advantage estimates. A learned critic evaluates the advantage of candidate actions across human demonstrations, autonomous rollouts, and real-time human interventions; the policy is then trained on this mixed corpus conditioned on target advantage and queried for high-advantage actions at run time. Rather than weighting gradients by the advantage or discarding failed trials, this formulation supplies an advantage indicator token as an additional conditioning input during supervised training so the network learns to associate suboptimal trajectories with negative advantage and successful recoveries with positive advantage, allowing runtime inference to prompt the policy with the positive-advantage indicator. Applied to generalist robot manipulation models, this approach more than doubled throughput and halved failure rates on the reported physical manipulation tasks (Physical Intelligence et al. 2025). However, because gradient updates modify every weight in the network, all nominal behaviors remain subject to drift.
