  • Policy-gradient fine-tuning in simulation applies reinforcement learning techniques (section 6.4.1) to update the weights of a pretrained vision-language-action policy using sparse or binary success rewards in parallel simulation (Li et al. 2025). Like advantage conditioning, this paradigm modifies all network parameters; in addition, it inherits every physical approximation and contact-solver idealization present in the underlying simulation environment.

Regression scope follows where the composite output can change and which execution assumptions the update affects. An independently enforced residual gate localizes the changed output, but regression still covers its active region, boundary transitions, combined closed-loop behavior, and timing and resource effects. Noise-space steering can change sampling throughout the base domain despite frozen decoder weights, so every affected operating mode requires regression unless a separate gate establishes a narrower scope. Full-weight updates can also alter behaviors across the entire operational envelope. A newly fine-tuned full-weight model demands complete end-to-end regression testing across all previously validated operational modes, and the system’s adaptation_wear_log must document every modified parameter group. Whether adaptation rollouts accumulate physical machine wear on hardware or incur modeling dependencies in simulation, each rollout must be recorded in the policy manifest. No model can legitimately claim expanded operational authority without concrete validation data recorded in the system audit log.

Bounded residual authority restricts the influence of an exploratory policy during execution, but it cannot alone prevent mechanical damage or eliminate latent vulnerabilities introduced during earlier training phases. Every stage of policy synthesis, from demonstration ingestion and simulation contact mechanics to residual adaptation, leaves a distinct behavioral signature on closed-loop execution. When an embodied machine develops high-frequency chattering, thermal derating, or semantic drift, systems engineers can trace that failure behavior directly back to the training assumptions, solver compromises, and architectural trade-offs that created it.

6.7 Training Failure Modes and Permission-Path Interceptors

A physical failure in a learned policy is rarely arbitrary. When an end-effector collides with a strike plate, that command reflects optimization over a specific dataset, loss formulation, and simulator dynamics, and the two failures that opened the chapter can now be traced back to theirs. The assumptions baked into that optimization leave a structural footprint on policy behavior, one that engineers can trace and mitigate before hardware ever runs. The policy vulnerability inference chain makes this causal trace explicit across five links: (\(\text{Exposure} \to \text{Dependency} \to \text{Violated Assumption} \to \text{Observable Signature} \to \text{Permission-Path Interceptor}\)). Training exposure establishes a learned dependency. An unmodeled deployment condition violates that dependency. The violation produces a measurable signature in the commands or physical plant telemetry. That signature names the specific runtime interceptor that the execution permission path requires. Reading the three latch checkpoints back through this chain shows how distinct optimization regimes produce different failure modes.

For latch-bc-01, behavioral cloning relies on the teleoperated openings. These demonstrations only support nominal plate alignment during the guarded approach; the data-collection campaign never demonstrated a displaced or misaligned plate. A sagging door frame directly violates that geometric dependency. The resulting failure signature is compounding covariate shift. Because behavioral cloning optimizes an open-loop supervised objective over expert trajectories, small drift errors compound at each control step. Individual motion proposals often remain physically plausible right up until the gripper mechanically binds against the frame. To prevent mechanical damage, a support monitor must evaluate runtime observations against the empirically supported demonstration cells and revoke policy authority outside them. Predeployment qualification follows directly from the known coverage gap that the scenario table of Choosing a tractable coverage table recorded: bench trials with deliberately displaced plates must confirm that the support monitor trips reliably before the mechanism binds.

For latch-sim-02, training inside a physics simulator instills strong dependencies on idealized contact dynamics and negligible actuation lag. A physical steel plate violates both assumptions, generating sharp contact forces and unmodeled transport delays that produce the overforce transients quantified in section 6.4.2. Paired contact probes expose this dynamical mismatch before hardware deployment. If the physical contact deadline leaves insufficient time for closed-loop deceleration, a reactive force tripwire arrives too late. Detecting a force excursion confirms that an impact occurred, but actuator inertia and mechanical compliance will continue driving the mechanism into the plate before the brakes can arrest motion. The interceptor is therefore the pre-contact stopping envelope of section 6.4.2, which guarantees a full stop at the standoff before the plate and hands engagement beyond it to the separately qualified guarded contact mode.

For latch-res-03, adaptation concentrates real-robot experience on the contact states of a single hardware rig. Localized fine-tuning targets tight-tolerance contact dynamics on that specific fixture, but it does not qualify the frozen base across the rest of the operational state space. Unlike full-weight fine-tuning, which can introduce catastrophic forgetting, this gated residual retains the base weights and holds corrections at zero in free space; its primary regression risks arise from contact corrections and gate-boundary transitions. Before updated weights receive operational authority, they must clear a frozen regression suite that evaluates both nominal latch engagement and free-space flight. In addition, an architectural residual gate must hold corrections at zero outside the designated contact phase, preventing fine-tuned offsets from destabilizing the free-space approach. Physical exploration during real-machine tuning (section 6.6) also imposes mechanical shock and motor winding heat. The runtime supervisor must monitor these thermal and structural thresholds continuously to ensure that task reward improvements do not mask hardware degradation.

Subsequent training stages do not automatically heal the unresolved dependencies of earlier stages. In the simulation-and-residual lineage (latch-sim-02 through latch-res-03), simulation artifacts persist until physical hardware evidence explicitly closes them. Conversely, the recovery-imitation branch adopts an alternative architectural path. The checkpoint latch-bc-recovery-02 incorporates targeted, human-guided recovery demonstrations for a named displaced-plate condition, expanding the operational envelope without relying on an uncalibrated simulator. Its remaining engineering uncertainties, chiefly closed-loop trajectory stability and potential regression on nominal openings, are made explicit through targeted qualification requests.

Ultimately, each inference chain connects a specific training assumption to an empirical test and an active execution limit. The policy manifest preserves these connections alongside the checkpoint’s source data, simulator configuration, optimization objective, and architectural constraints. By maintaining this end-to-end traceability, verification suites can systematically evaluate candidate policies against the exact physical vulnerabilities created by their training history.

6.8 The Policy Manifest

Handing a candidate checkpoint to an evaluation team with only its weight file and a training curve hides whether its behavior on a displaced plate comes from human recovery demonstrations, uncalibrated simulator rollouts, or pure network extrapolation. The candidate selection must distinguish nominal behavior supported by demonstrations, newly taught recovery, and contact behavior that still depends on unverified simulation. The failure chains of section 6.7 make that distinction concrete for each checkpoint; the record accompanying the candidate into evaluation preserves the reasoning, including the assumptions that remain open.

The policy manifest records how a checkpoint was produced (table 4). It lists the training stages and the data support the checkpoint inherits from the provenance records. It also lists the simulator versions, together with the omission log of mechanisms they leave out and mismatches paired probes have not closed. Finally, it records the wear incurred during adaptation and the operating domain the checkpoint declares. These fields state what evaluation must test and what runtime enforcement must accommodate. The runtime safety supervisor can check the manifest against its validated configuration before enabling a checkpoint. A declared boundary does not automatically generate a reliable detector or a safe clamp; those mechanisms require their own engineering and evidence.

The manifest separates optimization results from the conditions the policy may be asked to operate in. The declared ODD is the part of the ODD from Collection Policy Coverage that the policy manifest claims: the supported cells of the data join, minus every state the omission log excludes. High training reward or low offline loss does not show that the policy is safe outside it. The declared ODD is therefore computed from the join and the log, never from the training curve, and a convergent metric cannot stand in for clearance over regimes nobody characterized.

The separate latch-bc-recovery-02 manifest consumes the original provenance and the stipulated latch-recovery-02 archive, adding the positively displaced-plate cell at the guarded approach to the candidate domain with the expert recovery labels and held-out sessions identified by digest. Its qualification request asks whether the updated policy completes that recovery and preserves nominal service under the same physical limits. The original archive remains unchanged, and negative displacement and the fast approach remain excluded. Current provenance bounds the evidence already available, whereas collecting and qualifying new experience expands the machine’s useful competence.

For the original latch campaign and its simulation-residual lineage, a compact manifest links the fields into one decision:

  • regime_sequence: demonstration checkpoint latch-bc-01 → latch-sim-02, a student initialized from latch-bc-01 and distilled from a simulator teacher trained by reinforcement learning on privileged state (privileged contact wrenches \(\ContactWrench\) distilled into an 8-step observation-history buffer) → gated residual candidate latch-res-03. Retain each checkpoint hash, loss or reward specification, and stop rule.
  • base_checkpoint: no pretrained robot policy underlies the latch checkpoints, which descend from latch-bc-01 as regime_sequence records; any pretrained image encoder they use belongs here by digest. A candidate post-trained from the machine’s intent model would record that model’s weight digest, its pretraining mixture as a known unknown, and its action-space mapping, and would inherit the same declared ODD (section 6.2.8).
  • policy_inference_contract: specify checkpoint weights hash \(\text{hash}(\ModelParameters)\), the machine’s 80M-parameter ACT architecture with one decoder pass per chunk, chunk prediction horizon \(\ActionChunkHorizon =\) 16 setpoints at 25 ms, and the 40 ms p99 inference target. Record the selected execution prefix separately from the 50 ms proposal-renewal deadline and the selected chunk lease. Each renewed prefix requires valid evidence and a feasible continuation or fallback; the prediction horizon is not the active lease.
  • data_support: cite the latch campaign’s provenance records and its scenario table by digest; their join supports the nominal strike plate at the guarded approach, and lists the displaced plate and the faster approach, whose cells hold no demonstrations, as known absences with no recovery label.
  • simulation_provenance: record the actual solver build and contact parameters.
  • omission_log: the example simulator models strike-plate contact with a penalty spring far softer than the plate and omits actuator transport lag, so the contact states those two departures govern are unverified for the checkpoints trained in simulation.
  • negative_event_registry: cite by digest the authority-record entry, with its pre-trigger ring-buffer trace, for every fixture-shoulder strike, operator veto, or permission-path clamp with its event classification, without treating rejected learner commands as expert targets. Mark episodes an operator truncated, so that an update does not score an aborted command as a success.
  • adaptation_wear_log: associate latch-res-03 with the specific rig, above-threshold samples and separately identified contact events, motor-current history, modified residual weights, and free-space regression result.
  • declared_odd_boundary: the supported cells minus the states the omission log excludes, here the nominal plate at the guarded approach of 0.03 m/s. The contact states governed by the two omissions stay outside the declared ODD for every simulation-refined checkpoint, latch-sim-02 and latch-res-03 alike, until paired probes close them. The displaced plate and the 0.10 m/s approach lie outside it, and the fast approach would stay outside even with demonstrations, because its contact deadline (section 6.4.2) leaves almost nothing beyond the contact-loop response. There the permission path must impose the guarded approach or refuse policy authority.

A qualification review would withhold operational authority from latch-res-03 until paired contact probes quantify the contact-stiffness error and the omitted lag, and the combined controller passes contact, free-space, stopping, and wear-budget tests. With only the original campaign, the same rule leaves latch-bc-01 as the checkpoint evaluation may sample, provided the support monitor of its chain refuses policy authority off the nominal plate (section 6.7). Trained without a simulator, it carries no omission-log exclusions, so its declared ODD keeps the door’s contact states at the nominal plate and the guarded approach. The manifest records that decision and its evidence rather than converting a training score into permission.

The manifest references the collector, plant, calibration, and action taps through the provenance record of The Dataset Schema. The cited digests preserve which data and scenario table produced the checkpoint; later changes require a new record rather than silently updating its lineage. Simulator provenance similarly fixes the solver and timestep12 used during optimization. Its separate omission log carries unresolved discrepancies from section 6.5 into evaluation’s coverage gaps and fault-injection tests.

12 Penalty-Contact Timestep: The contact timescale scales as \(\sqrt{\EffectiveImpactMass/\EffectiveSeriesStiffness}\). For the latch’s 9 kg moving mass it is about 24 ms against the soft penalty spring and 4.7 ms against the real plate, so a timestep sized to resolve the simulated contact is about 5× too coarse once the spring is stiffened to match the plate. The integrator, damping, and solver tolerances set the final step, which is refined until contact onset, peak force, and impulse converge.

Downstream engineering teams consume the manifest throughout the design, evaluation, and release lifecycle. The evaluation record of The Evaluation Record consumes it directly. Its target ODD is sampled from inside the declared ODD, and its catalog of coverage gaps inherits the known absences and the omission log, so evaluation suites probe the specific failure modes each training regime predicts. Targeted exploratory tests outside the candidate domain can also identify gaps, but they do not establish an operational claim there without new support and qualification. The permission path takes the declared ODD as the outer limit of the region in which it admits policy proposals, and uses the recorded support to parameterize out-of-distribution detectors. Verification and the safety case draw on the manifest to establish the provenance of every parameter update and to confirm that each omission is bounded by an independent physical interlock.

↪ The omission log becomes one of the upstream records from which fault records are compiled for release, developed in Fault Records for Release.

Checkpoint 6.3: Policy manifests and declared ODDs

Before releasing a trained policy checkpoint to physical verification, confirm the following relationships between the policy manifest, its omission log, and the declared ODD:

The manifest turns a training history into a claim with stated limits. The original record sends latch-bc-01 to evaluation and keeps latch-res-03 out of service until paired probes and regression tests close its gaps. The stipulated recovery record sends latch-bc-recovery-02 with a wider, explicitly testable candidate claim. The common ways that decision goes wrong all let a training score stand in for that evidence.

6.9 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Minimizing loss on an offline demonstration split or maximizing cumulative return in simulation measures compliance with training assumptions rather than physical competence on hardware. When an objective rewards behavior that exploits a curated demonstration distribution or a simulator approximation, the ranking of candidates established in training can invert on deployment. The candidate that scored highest in simulation finishes lowest on hardware, through unsupported state drift, contact-force spikes past the structural limit, or torque oscillations that grow into resonant chatter (figure 9). Virtual environments forgive maneuvers that physical hardware penalizes with tripwires, resonance, or thermal overload, so the causal assumptions connecting a training metric to physical execution have to be tested before a policy receives actuator authority.

Pitfall: Sanitizing recovery episodes to achieve lower offline validation loss.

Suppose an archive of latch manipulation demonstrations includes human recovery maneuvers, and two separate policies train on competing subsets of this corpus. One policy trains on the full archive, including episodes where a human teleoperator backed off the gripper and re-aligned the jaws after the latch cam caught on the strike plate. The second policy trains on a sanitized subset stripped of all hesitation and retries, achieving a markedly lower loss on its held-out validation split. When deployed on the physical cell, both policies eventually encounter a misaligned cam. The first policy recognizes the contact anomaly, backs off, and seats the latch successfully. The second policy relentlessly drives along the nominal path, building contact force until the safety supervisor’s force tripwire halts the cell. Because the sanitized dataset excluded off-nominal recoveries, the lower validation loss proves only that predicting smooth nominal trajectories is easier to fit than handling physical disturbances. Pruning corrective demonstrations simplifies the loss score at the exact price of removing the feedback behaviors the physical controller needs off the nominal path. Filtering out recovery trajectories to drive down offline prediction loss strips the policy of the corrective demonstrations required to escape compounding physical errors.

Architect’s countermeasure: Retain all authority-record entries during dataset ingestion and admit, as training targets, only the stabilized, human-reviewed segments of attributable expert recoveries. Never prune demonstration corpora to minimize offline validation loss, and mandate closed-loop physical perturbation benchmarks to verify recovery competence before signing off on deployment candidate checkpoints.

Fallacy: Transitioning from human demonstrations to simulation reinforcement learning removes the failures training left in the policy.

An assembly team switches from behavioral cloning to simulation reinforcement learning to eliminate lateral drift during automated gasket seating. The resulting policy cures the drift in the virtual environment but commands joint torque oscillations at 65 Hz. The physics simulator permits this aggressive chatter because its rigid-body solver assumes massless motor rotors and instantaneous torque reversal. On the physical machine, this high-frequency command excites drivetrain mechanical resonance and rapidly heats the motor windings toward their thermal trip point via \(\TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \ElectricalResistance\) Joule losses. The drive electronics throttle current to prevent permanent motor damage, causing the assembly stroke to stall before seating completes. Shifting training regimes solved an imitation drift problem by introducing a high-frequency chatter exploit that the simulator did not penalize. Qualification must therefore evaluate the full physical task under sustained cyclic operation, verifying simulator dynamics assumptions against physical hardware telemetry. Switching to simulation reinforcement learning substitutes numerical solver shortcuts and unmodeled mechanical dynamics for human demonstration deficits.

Architect’s countermeasure: Require paired-trace validation across all four reality gap channels before deploying simulation-trained policies. Enforce low-level hardware rate limiters and digital low-pass action filters (\(\ActionFilterCutoffFrequency \le\) 20 Hz) within the embedded motor drives to suppress high-frequency chatter originating from unmodeled rotor inertia.

Fallacy: Assuming online gradient updates preserve predeployment safety envelopes.

When a simulation-trained policy shows steady-state positioning error on a physical assembly fixture, an engineer might enable online policy-gradient fine-tuning directly on the robot. After 5,000 gradient steps, the arm successfully eliminates contact positioning error at the fixture, prompting the team to declare the updated model production-ready. Applying parameter updates \(\FiniteChange\ModelParameters\) directly to the policy network, however, produces an entirely new checkpoint with changed weights. The behavioral trials accumulated during predeployment qualification remain observations of the original checkpoint and do not establish performance of the updated one. Independent permission and fallback properties may remain applicable only while their plant, timing, authority, isolation, and realization premises hold; integration must check those premises after the update. While local contact accuracy improves, unconstrained backpropagation into shared representations triggers catastrophic forgetting in rarely visited state-space regions. As a result, the fine-tuned policy may lose stability during high-speed free-space slews or corrupt its emergency deceleration profiles. Because physical robot time and mechanical wear preclude repeating full-scale testing suites on hardware, regression suites must isolate and verify every operational envelope that gradient updates might perturb. Online gradient updates require fresh behavioral qualification and reopen every assurance claim that depends on the changed checkpoint or execution conditions.

Architect’s countermeasure: When a bounded correction suffices, use gated residual policy architectures (\(\NominalControlCommand = \pi_{\text{base}} + \ResidualGate(\DecisionProcessState)\,\FiniteChange\Policy\)) with an externally enforced binary gate (\(\ResidualGate(\DecisionProcessState) \equiv 0\) in free space) to localize real-world adaptation. Mandate automated, frozen non-regression suites that verify trajectory tracking and stopping profiles before granting execution authority to updated parameters.

Fallacy: Believing that widening parameter distributions can substitute for missing physical mechanisms.

When an assembly policy repeatedly fails on physical hardware, an engineering team might attempt to brute-force sim-to-real transfer by radically widening the simulator’s domain randomization bounds. They randomize link masses by ±50 percent and vary surface friction coefficients across the extreme interval from 0.05 to 1.5. On the physical test bench, however, the trained policy behaves poorly: it moves sluggishly through free space and hesitates indefinitely upon initial contact. Randomization perturbs coefficients exclusively within the governing differential equations already hardcoded in the physics engine. It cannot synthesize missing physical phenomena such as gearbox backlash, joint cable compliance, Stribeck friction, or sensor transport jitter. When dynamically incompatible virtual plants produce indistinguishable observations, a memoryless policy cannot choose separately for each plant; average-return optimization may favor a slow compromise or may sacrifice low-probability cases. While recurrent or history-conditioned architectures can adapt to wide parametric variations by estimating system properties online, even an adaptive policy cannot identify or compensate for physical mechanisms that never existed in its training simulator. Expanding parameter randomization bounds merely shifts the probability distribution across existing simulator equations; it cannot synthesize unmodeled physical dynamics.

Architect’s countermeasure: Calibrate baseline physics engine parameters against real hardware test fixtures before introducing domain randomization. Explicitly document whether parameter distributions derive from empirical instrument measurements or heuristic curricula such as automatic domain randomization. Enumerate known physical omissions, including transmission elasticity and actuator deadbands, in the policy manifest’s omission log, ensuring that hardware-level safety watchdogs bound operations within verified modeling assumptions.

Fallacy: Assuming multi-step generative decoders can execute synchronously within inner actuator control loops.

Aiming to capture multimodal trajectory distributions observed in offline demonstration logs, an engineer replaces the single-pass ACT feedforward decoder in latch-bc-01 with an iterative 16-step diffusion denoising head. The team deploys the new architecture synchronously into the robot’s real-time inner control loop, scheduling a complete iterative sampling cycle for every 25 ms actuator setpoint. Under the hardware inference budget evaluated in section 6.2.6, executing all denoising steps consumes 48 ms, exceeding the allowable control step deadline. Because the synchronous dispatch pipeline stalls awaiting model convergence, the system routinely misses setpoint deadlines. The low-level controller must either freeze on stale command setpoints or trip safety watchdogs on every iteration overrun. The weight-streaming floor of The memory wall bounds each evaluation from below, and Action chunking shows that re-reading the action head on every evaluation can dominate the decoder’s cost. Each of the 16 dependent evaluations pays that floor, so the sum misses the setpoint deadline before scheduling jitter is even counted. Deploying multi-step generative policies into high-rate actuator loops requires decoupling iterative trajectory generation from real-time setpoint tracking through asynchronous execution contracts.

Architect’s countermeasure: Decouple iterative policy inference from high-frequency actuator control loops whenever measured inference latency approaches the control period. Formally partition the pipeline into an asynchronous planner generating trajectory chunks and an inner controller tracking them under qualified execution leases. Explicitly track prediction horizons, executed setpoint prefixes, and proposal renewal deadlines. Buffering trajectory chunks never grants permanent operational authority: the execution engine continuously validates physical safety invariants and triggers safe stopping profiles whenever a lease expires without a fresh proposal.

6.10 Summary

A policy’s weights keep what its training experience rewarded and forget which experience that was, so each regime is best remembered by the dependency it leaves behind. Demonstrations leave a dependency on coverage: a point-regression head averages valid modes into an action no one chose, and even a multimodal head drifts into states the demonstrations never recovered from. A reward-labeled log leaves a dependency on the support of the logged actions, and a pretrained base on someone else’s embodiment. Trial on the machine pays for its experience in calendar time, winding heat, and drivetrain life. Simulation pays almost nothing per trial but inherits every approximation in its contact solver and every mechanism it omits. Fine-tuning on the machine creates a new checkpoint that needs its own behavioral evidence and review of affected assurance premises. None of these dependencies shows in a validation loss or a simulated return.

Orthogonal to the source of experience, every policy must deliver its proposal inside the machine’s deadline \(\CompletionDeadline\). The decoder that represents several modes buys that representation with dependent evaluations, and what decides whether it can run is renewal slack against the jitter allowance, not mean latency. When slack runs out, the chunk lease, not the decoder, bounds how long an admitted prefix keeps moving the arm.

The policy manifest gathers these dependencies into one claim with stated limits. Its declared ODD is the supported cells of the data join minus every state the omission log excludes, never a region read off a training curve. For the latch, that rule sends latch-bc-01 to evaluation at the nominal plate and the guarded approach. It holds the simulation-refined checkpoints until paired probes bound the contact and timing omissions. It also lets latch-bc-recovery-02 carry a wider claim that new recovery experience has to earn. An admitted action crossing the causal boundary carries no guarantee beyond the evidence that qualifies it, and the load-bearing relations behind that evidence are collected in table 5.

Key Takeaways: Policy synthesis and sim-to-real transfer
  • Every regime leaves a dependency no training score shows: Offline loss and simulated return reward compliance with training data and numerical solvers rather than real-world physics. Candidate model rankings frequently invert when transferred to hardware. When an operating condition violates an unmodeled dependency, it produces a predictable failure signature that identifies exactly which runtime monitor the permission path must enforce.
  • Mode choice and recovery are separate problems: Point regression can average valid, mutually exclusive demonstrations into an unviable intermediate action, whereas compounding execution drift pushes the system into states that the demonstrations never sampled. Multimodal decoders resolve action ambiguity; targeted recovery experience and closed-loop data resolve distributional drift. Independent runtime limits remain mandatory for both.
  • Decoder latency must fit the proposal contract: Iterative denoising and deep autoregressive evaluations substantially increase compute latency and memory bandwidth pressure. When a policy cannot generate actions within the synchronous deadline, asynchronous action-chunk playback requires a validated hardware lease and deterministic fallback behavior. An expressive action distribution earns no extra execution time at the actuator.
  • Training information must survive the sensor boundary: Privileged simulation states, such as ground-truth contact wrenches or global object poses, accelerate training, but the deployed student policy observes only noisy onboard sensor streams. Observation histories disambiguate physical variations only when those conditions leave distinct temporal signatures. Model distillation never removes the necessity for hardware calibration and empirical transfer testing.
  • Randomization covers parameters, not missing mechanisms: Domain randomization widens policy robustness across modeled simulator parameters, but it cannot bridge physical effects that the numerical solver neglects. Backlash, unmodeled compliance, and sensor transport delays remain absent regardless of parameter range, requiring explicit entries in the omission log and channel-by-channel probe characterization on hardware.
  • Fine-tuning spends wear and reopens qualification: On-robot trials consume component life, introduce thermal stress, and generate wear that cannot be parallelized across separate test rigs. Behavioral evidence gathered for an earlier checkpoint does not qualify an updated model, and independent permission properties require an integration check of their unchanged premises. A gated residual architecture strictly confines where learned adaptations take effect, but the integrated controller still demands thorough regression testing and hardware-level execution limits.
  • A wider declared ODD needs new support: The policy manifest records dataset digests, training hyperparameters, and unmodeled simulator phenomena, explicitly excluding operational domains affected by unverified gaps. Scaling simulator throughput or pretraining a foundation model adds no supported cell to the declared ODD. Broadening the deployment boundary requires attributable physical recovery data and rigorous closed-loop hardware qualification.

The declared ODD is where the second law, endogenous data (principle 2), becomes a field in a record. A policy chooses its next observations by acting, so the states it meets in service are not the states its training visited, and no regime in this chapter escapes that. Cloning meets it as drift out of the demonstrated tube, learning by trial on the machine meets it as calendar time and wear spent visiting the policy’s own states, and simulation meets it as states whose dynamics the solver invented. The manifest does not remove the dependency. It writes down where the policy’s own evidence ends, so that everything beyond that line is treated as a claim still to be earned rather than as competence the weights happen to display.

When a candidate looks better than its evidence, the useful question is which of its states were visited on this machine and which were only scored. A wider declared ODD is bought with experience that joins to new cells, and choosing a training regime is choosing what that experience costs: an operator’s hours, a drivetrain’s life, or a probe campaign that closes a simulator’s omissions.

6.11 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The mathematical foundations of modern policy optimization originate in classical dynamic programming. Bellman (1957) introduced the principle of optimality and the recursive decomposition that defines value functions, while Howard (1960) formulated policy iteration to solve discrete Markov decision processes. Parallel to dynamic programming, learning from demonstration developed along an empirical path in robotics. Pomerleau’s ALVINN demonstrated early autonomous road following by training a shallow artificial neural network to map camera images directly to steering angles (Pomerleau 1989). ALVINN quickly exposed the vulnerability of pure behavioral cloning to covariate shift and compounding error. The network trained exclusively on demonstrations from an attentive human driver, so it never encountered the off-center heading angles produced by its own steering mistakes, which led to catastrophic departures from the roadway (Pomerleau 1991). Ross et al. (2011) formalized this failure mode through the Dataset Aggregation (DAgger) algorithm, proving no-regret reduction bounds by querying an expert supervisor on the actual state distribution induced by the learner’s policy. When demonstrations encompass multiple valid strategies, naive regression averages distinct modes into invalid intermediate actions. Mixture density networks (Bishop 1994) resolved this multimodality for low-dimensional outputs, and diffusion policies (Chi et al. 2025) generalized the approach to high-dimensional action trajectories, executing action chunks with receding horizons on physical manipulators.

Pomerleau, Dean A. 1989. “ALVINN: An Autonomous Land Vehicle in a Neural Network.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 1: 305–13.
Pomerleau, Dean A. 1991. “Efficient Training of Artificial Neural Networks for Autonomous Navigation.” Neural Computation 3 (1): 88–97. https://doi.org/10.1162/neco.1991.3.1.88.
Watkins, Christopher J. C. H., and Peter Dayan. 1992. “Q-Learning.” Machine Learning 8 (3–4): 279–92. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF00992698.
Levine, Sergey, Peter Pastor, Alex Krizhevsky, Julian Ibarz, and Deirdre Quillen. 2018. “Learning Hand-Eye Coordination for Robotic Grasping with Deep Learning and Large-Scale Data Collection.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 37 (4-5): 421–36.
Kalashnikov, Dmitry, Alex Irpan, Peter Pastor, Julian Ibarz, Alexander Herzog, Eric Jang, Deirdre Quillen, et al. 2018. “Scalable Deep Reinforcement Learning for Vision-Based Robotic Manipulation.” Proceedings of the 2nd Conference on Robot Learning, Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 87: 651–73.
Schulman, John, Filip Wolski, Prafulla Dhariwal, Alec Radford, and Oleg Klimov. 2017. “Proximal Policy Optimization Algorithms.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1707.06347.
Makoviychuk, Viktor, Lukasz Wawrzyniak, Yunrong Guo, Michelle Lu, Kier Storey, Miles Macklin, David Hoeller, et al. 2021. “Isaac Gym: High Performance GPU-Based Physics Simulation for Robot Learning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2108.10470.
Rudin, Nikita, David Hoeller, Philipp Reist, and Marco Hutter. 2022. “Learning to Walk in Minutes Using Massively Parallel Deep Reinforcement Learning.” Proceedings of the 5th Conference on Robot Learning, Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 164: 91–100.

Algorithmic reinforcement learning split into two primary branches: policy-gradient methods originating with REINFORCE (Williams 1992) and value-based temporal-difference learning formalizing around Q-learning (Watkins and Dayan 1992). Both paradigms measure sample complexity in abstract environmental transitions, ignoring the physical costs of real-time execution. On a real robot, every trial consumes calendar time, strains mechanical linkages, overheats actuators, and demands human intervention for environment resets. Large-scale physical grasping campaigns (Levine et al. 2018; Kalashnikov et al. 2018) demonstrated that collecting hundreds of thousands of real-world interactions requires dedicated robot farms running continuously for months under heavy maintenance. To bypass this physical bottleneck, robotic learning shifted sample generation into simulation. Proximal Policy Optimization (Schulman et al. 2017) established itself as the standard policy learner by clipping the update objective to discourage excessive policy drift. Simultaneously, physics engines co-located directly on hardware accelerators eliminated host-to-device communication bottlenecks, enabling thousands of parallel rollouts on a single workstation and delivering billions of simulated timesteps per day (Makoviychuk et al. 2021; Rudin et al. 2022).

Simulated rollouts solved the sample-throughput bottleneck, but they elevated the reality gap into the central engineering challenge. Policies trained inside idealized physics often fail catastrophically when deployed against the unmodeled friction, latency, and compliance of physical hardware. Domain randomization addressed this discrepancy by treating the real world as an unobserved draw from a randomized distribution of simulated environments, perturbing visual features (Tobin et al. 2017) and inertial dynamics (Peng et al. 2018). The OpenAI Dactyl project (Andrychowicz et al. 2020) showed that broad dynamics randomization paired with a recurrent policy could reorient a block in a dexterous hand trained entirely in simulation, while reporting the unsimulated failures of the physical hand, chiefly hardware breakage under load. Its successor extended the approach to solving a Rubik’s cube with automatic domain randomization (OpenAI et al. 2019). Classical system identification (Ljung 1999) found new life inside neural pipelines through learned actuator networks that capture non-ideal actuator dynamics and drive compliance (Hwangbo et al. 2019). More recently, generalist foundation policies trained across diverse robotic platforms (Kim et al. 2024; Black et al. 2024) use pooled fleet demonstrations to cross-train visual and motor representations. However, borrowing cross-embodiment data introduces unverified physical assumptions and subtle distributional shifts. The policy manifest introduced in this chapter provides the rigorous systems contract required to track these lineage dependencies, documenting simulation distributions, sensor bounds, and training assumptions so that hardware deployment can be audited and verified.

OpenAI, Ilge Akkaya, Marcin Andrychowicz, Maciek Chociej, Mateusz Litwin, Bob McGrew, Arthur Petron, et al. 2019. “Solving Rubik’s Cube with a Robot Hand.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1910.07113.
Hwangbo, Jemin, Joonho Lee, Alexey Dosovitskiy, Dario Bellicoso, Vassilios Tsounis, Vladlen Koltun, and Marco Hutter. 2019. “Learning Agile and Dynamic Motor Skills for Legged Robots.” Science Robotics 4 (26): eaau5872. https://doi.org/10.1126/scirobotics.aau5872.
Kim, Moo Jin, Karl Pertsch, Siddharth Karamcheti, Ted Xiao, Ashwin Balakrishna, Suraj Nair, Rafael Rafailov, et al. 2024. “OpenVLA: An Open-Source Vision-Language-Action Model.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2406.09246.
Black, Kevin, Noah Brown, Danny Driess, Adnan Esmail, Michael Equi, Chelsea Finn, Niccolo Fusai, et al. 2024. “\(\pi_0\): A Vision-Language-Action Flow Model for General Robot Control.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2410.24164.

Six foundational works trace the trajectory from classical dynamic programming to modern sim-to-real policy deployment:

  • Sutton and Barto (2018) provides the definitive derivation of Markov decision processes, value functions, and policy-gradient theorems from first principles, serving as the core reference for the reinforcement learning formulations in section 6.2.1.
  • Ross et al. (2011) establishes the theoretical bound on compounding error in sequential prediction, introduces the DAgger reduction algorithm, and outlines the recoverability assumptions that section 6.2.4 evaluates on physical robot hardware.
  • Chi et al. (2025) introduces score-based diffusion models for action sequence generation on physical manipulators, establishing the receding-horizon chunk execution and iterative denoising computational budgets analyzed in section 6.2.6.
  • Peng et al. (2018) focuses domain randomization on unmodeled physical dynamics rather than visual textures, demonstrating that recurrent policies implicitly identify system dynamics across randomized friction, mass, and damping distributions to achieve robust sim-to-real transfer.
  • Andrychowicz et al. (2020) offers one of the most comprehensive engineering case studies of an end-to-end sim-to-real deployment, detailing the distributed training pipeline, dynamics randomization ablations, and physical maintenance challenges encountered while reorienting a block with an anthropomorphic hand.
  • Ljung (1999) provides the definitive treatise on classical system identification, detailing the input-output parameter estimation techniques, excitation design, and validation metrics that ground modern actuator modeling and paired physical probing.
Sutton, Richard S., and Andrew G. Barto. 2018. Reinforcement Learning: An Introduction. 2nd ed. MIT Press.
Ross, Stéphane, Geoffrey Gordon, and J. Andrew Bagnell. 2011. “A Reduction of Imitation Learning and Structured Prediction to No-Regret Online Learning.” Proceedings of the Fourteenth International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS) 15: 627–35.
Chi, Cheng, Zhenjia Xu, Siyuan Feng, Eric Cousineau, Yilun Du, Benjamin Burchfiel, Russ Tedrake, and Shuran Song. 2025. “Diffusion Policy: Visuomotor Policy Learning via Action Diffusion.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 44 (10-11): 1684–704. https://doi.org/10.1177/02783649241273668.
Andrychowicz, Marcin, Bowen Baker, Maciek Chociej, Rafał J’ozefowicz, et al. 2020. “Learning Dexterous in-Hand Manipulation.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 39 (1): 3–20.

What’s Next: From policy manifests to evidence
Twenty successful consecutive trials provide limited statistical confidence for a demonstration checkpoint whose manifest declares a single nominal cell and inherits none of the simulator’s omissions. Closed-Loop Evaluation takes the formal policy manifest as its contractual input. It opens with an illustrative campaign of twenty nominal door trials of latch-bc-01, the single cell its manifest declares, and evaluates the bound so small a physical sample can establish. It then evaluates latch-bc-recovery-02 through nominal regression and displaced-plate trials to decide whether the new recovery experience earns the wider claim its manifest requests within the guarded approach.
